So I’ll usually end up going to the bathroom and making myself throw up, because it feels like the only way to undo what just happened.\n\nThere have been times where I haven’t, or I’ve tried not to, but most of the time after those episodes, that’s where my mind goes almost automatically. It’s like the eating part ends, and then the bathroom part becomes the next thing I feel like I have to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about the possibility of gaining weight when you're considering making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much the main thing I’m thinking about, honestly.\n\nIt’s not always like a clear sentence in my head, like “I’m going to gain weight unless I do this,” but that’s the fear underneath it. If I’ve eaten too much, my brain goes straight to, “This is going to show up on me,” or “I’m going to get fat,” and then I feel like I have to do something to stop it.\n\nEven outside of those moments, I get really caught up in small changes. Like if I get on the scale after a work shift and it’s even a pound higher, or if my work pants feel tighter than usual, I’ll spend the whole day worrying about it. I’ll be thinking, “Okay, I’m letting myself go,” or “I need to make up for this somehow.” And then I start planning what I should do differently, or how to fix it.\n\nSo when I’m considering making myself throw up, yeah, gaining weight is definitely part of what I’m afraid of. It feels like I’m trying to undo it before it becomes real.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Usually about once a week… I mean, for at least the last four months, there’s generally been one night most weeks where I eat way more than I meant to when I’m by myself, and then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nSome weeks it might happen more than once, but if I’m trying not to exaggerate, the steady pattern is at least once a week. It’s usually at night, when I’m alone, and once I’ve eaten that much, it feels like I almost automatically go into that next step. I don’t really like admitting how regular it’s become, but that’s the most honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used laxatives or diuretics to try to undo the effects of eating?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, not in the way you’re asking. I haven’t really used laxatives or water pills to try to fix it.\n\nFor me, it’s more that after I eat like that, I usually end up going to the bathroom and making myself throw up. It feels very immediate, like I have to undo it right then, before the calories settle in or before it “counts.” I know that sounds strange saying it out loud, but in that moment it feels like the only option my brain gives me.\n\nDoctor: Is fear of gaining weight something that you think about often, even when you're not eating or considering making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not only right after I’ve eaten or when I’m thinking about throwing up. It’s kind of just running in the background a lot of the time.\n\nLike, if I weigh myself after a shift and the number is even a pound higher, I can get stuck on it for the rest of the day. I’ll tell myself maybe it’s just water or that I’ve been on my feet all day, but it doesn’t really calm me down. My mind goes straight to, “You’re letting yourself get fat,” or “You need to get this under control.”\n\nOr if my work pants feel tighter than usual, even just a little, I notice it constantly. I’ll be bending down or walking around and all I can think about is how they feel on me. Then I start planning how I’m going to make up for it, like what I should do differently or how I can fix it. It’s embarrassing because it can take over the whole day from something that small.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight influence what you eat or how you behave on a typical day when you're not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. Even on days when I’m not having one of those episodes, it can shape the whole day more than I want to admit.\n\nLike if I get on the scale after work and it’s even one pound higher, I don’t just notice it and move on. I’ll keep thinking about it while I’m showering, while I’m trying to relax, even when I’m getting ready for bed. My brain starts going, “You’re letting yourself get fat,” and then I start mentally reviewing everything I ate, or planning how I’m going to make up for it.\n\nSame thing with my work pants. If they feel tighter, even if it’s probably just because I’ve been moving around all day or they came out of the wash weird, I can’t stop noticing it. It makes me feel really uncomfortable in my body, and then I start thinking I need to be stricter or do something to fix it. So, yeah… it affects what I eat and how I act, even on a normal day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever restrict what you eat or skip meals on days when you're not having one of these episodes because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I do start thinking in terms of “making up for it,” even on days when I haven’t had one of those episodes.\n\nIt can start with something really small, like if I weigh myself after a shift and it’s even a pound higher than I expected. I know logically that could be anything — water, being on my feet all day, whatever — but emotionally it feels like proof that I’m losing control. Then I’ll spend the rest of the day kind of obsessing over it, like, “Okay, what do I need to do differently now?” or “How do I fix this before it gets worse?”\n\nOr if my work pants feel tighter, I get really stuck on that. I’ll notice it every time I move, and then my head goes straight to, “You’re letting yourself get fat.” And once that thought is there, it’s hard not to start planning around food — like being stricter, cutting back, trying to compensate somehow. It’s not always some organized plan, but the fear definitely pushes me to change what I’m eating or think about skipping or limiting things because I feel like I need to undo whatever is happening.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect your relationships with friends and family or your ability to work and participate in activities you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does, especially with work. I don’t always want to admit that because I like being seen as someone who’s competent, you know? Like I can handle my job and be professional. But it definitely gets in the way.\n\nThere was this bilingual outreach presentation at work — it was one of those things where I’m supposed to be focused and present and making sure people understand what we’re explaining. And the whole time, or at least a big part of it, I was distracted thinking about how I could leave afterward. Not even in a normal “I’m tired and want to go home” way, more like I was already mentally trying to get out of there because I felt so uncomfortable and anxious about my body and weight.\n\nAnd because my mind was somewhere else, I stumbled through parts of the presentation. I lost my place, repeated myself, and I could feel myself getting flustered. Then of course that made me more self-conscious, because I started worrying that my coworkers noticed I wasn’t really on top of it. I kept thinking, “They can tell something’s wrong,” or “They’re going to think I’m not prepared,” when really I had prepared — I just couldn’t stay focused.\n\nSo, yeah, it affects things. Maybe not every single day in some dramatic way, but enough that it’s interfering with my ability to do my job the way I know I can.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight something that you think about every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, yes. It’s pretty much every day, even if the intensity changes.\n\nSome days it’s not like I’m sitting there actively panicking the whole time, but it’s still kind of running in the background. Like, if I get home from a shift and the scale is even one pound higher, that can just take over my head. I’ll tell myself, “It’s probably water or being on my feet all day,” but then another part of me is already going, “No, you’re letting yourself get fat.” And then I’m thinking about it while I’m doing other things, like replaying what I ate or trying to figure out how I’m going to make up for it.\n\nOr with my work pants — if they feel tighter, that can ruin my whole day. I’ll keep noticing it every time I bend down or walk around, and it turns into this constant reminder that something is wrong with me or that I need to fix it. So even if nothing major happens that day, the fear is still there, and it affects how I think about food and my body almost daily.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social activities or events that you avoid because of your concerns about weight or body image?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, sometimes it’s not even that I’m consciously saying, “I can’t go because of my body,” but that’s what it turns into.\n\nLike, I’ll be getting ready in the bathroom at my apartment, and I’ll catch myself in the mirror, and then everything kind of narrows down to my stomach and my waist. It doesn’t matter if I had a good day otherwise — like even if I did well at the rescue center, handled the animals, got through everything I needed to do — if I look in the mirror and my waist or stomach looks wrong to me, it feels like I failed. Like I wasn’t disciplined enough.\n\nAnd once that feeling hits, going out feels… exposed, I guess. Like I don’t want to be around people or have them see me, even if realistically they’re not looking that closely. It just makes me want to stay home instead of going to something social, because I’m already so stuck in my head about how I look.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the effects of eating after these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that is the main reason. It’s not like I’m doing it because I feel physically sick or something. It’s more like after I eat that way, my mind goes straight to, “You have to undo this before it counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nI usually end up in the bathroom making myself throw up, because in that moment it feels like the only way to stop the calories from… I don’t know, settling in or becoming part of me. I know that probably sounds strange, but it feels really urgent at the time. Like if I don’t do it quickly, then I’ve lost control completely and I’m going to gain weight from it.\n\nSo yes, the fear of gaining weight is a huge part of it. It’s not the only emotion — there’s guilt and panic and shame too — but the reason I try to get rid of it is mostly because I’m scared of what eating like that will do to my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you currently weigh less than 85% of the expected weight for your age and height?\n\nPatient: I’m… not actually sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t really know what the “expected weight” would be for my age and height, or what 85% of that would come out to.\n\nI do weigh myself, but I don’t think about it in those terms, like percentages. I’d probably need you to tell me what number you mean or calculate it. I can tell you my current weight if that helps, but I don’t know off the top of my head whether it’s below that cutoff.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me your current weight?\n\nPatient: I… honestly don’t know the exact number right now. I know that probably sounds like I should, given how much I think about it, but I’m not sure I can give you a current weight that I’d trust.\n\nI do weigh myself sometimes, but it’s not always consistent, and the number kind of gets stuck in my head afterward, so I don’t always want to look directly at it or say it out loud. I’d rather not guess and give you something inaccurate. If you need it, I could check or get weighed here, but off the top of my head, I’m not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

It feels like I’m trying to undo it before it becomes real.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Usually about once a week… I mean, for at least the last four months, there’s generally been one night most weeks where I eat way more than I meant to when I’m by myself, and then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nSome weeks it might happen more than once, but if I’m trying not to exaggerate, the steady pattern is at least once a week. Part of me is literally thinking, “Stop, put the food away, this is enough,” but my hands are still getting more, or I’m opening something else. It feels like I lose the ability to decide in that moment, especially when I’m alone and there’s no one around to interrupt it or see it happening. That’s the main reason I’m here, because it’s started to scare me how out of control it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating by yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I don’t walk away feeling good about it, obviously, but “guilty” is hard because sometimes it’s more like I feel confused or embarrassed, like I’m trying to replay how it even got to that point. I’ll sort of sit there afterward and think, “Why did I do that again?” but I don’t know if that’s guilt exactly or just frustration with myself.\n\nThere’s definitely this heavy feeling after, like I don’t really want to think about it and I don’t want anyone to know. But I don’t always label it as guilt in my head. It’s more like… I feel off, and kind of ashamed maybe, but I’m not sure if I’m using the right word.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s probably the clearest part of it, honestly. Once I’ve started, especially if I’m alone at night, it feels like the stopping part just sort of disappears. I can be thinking very clearly, like, “Put this away now, you’ve had enough, stop opening things,” and at the same time I’m still eating or getting more food.\n\nThat’s what scares me, because it doesn’t feel like a normal decision where I’m just choosing to have extra. It feels more like something has taken over the process, and I’m watching myself keep going even though I don’t want to. I’ll make little rules in my head, like “after this bite” or “after this one thing,” but then I move the line again, and again, and suddenly I’ve eaten way more than I intended.\n\nSo, yeah… I do feel unable to stop once it starts. Not every single time I eat, but during those episodes, yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I know that’s probably something I should be tracking more carefully, but I don’t have a clear answer like, “I gained this many pounds” or “it changed at this exact time.” I notice things more vaguely, like how clothes feel or how I feel looking in the mirror, and even that can be hard to judge because some days I feel like I look completely different than other days.\n\nI don’t think I can confidently say there’s been a significant change that I know is because of these episodes. My weight might move around a bit, but I haven’t been keeping consistent track, and I try not to focus on the number too much because then I can get kind of stuck on it. So… I guess the honest answer is I haven’t noticed a big, clear change over time, at least not one I can point to for sure.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating by yourself happen?\n\nPatient: Usually it’s at least once a week… like, for the last four months or so, I’d say there’s been at least one night most weeks where it happens.\n\nIt’s almost always when I’m alone, and it tends to be at night. I’ll end up eating way more than I meant to — not just “I had a big snack,” but like I keep going past the point I planned to stop. And then afterward I make myself throw up. I don’t like saying that part out loud, but that’s been the pattern.\n\nSome weeks it might be more than once, but if I’m trying to give you the most accurate answer, at minimum it’s usually one night a week for the past several months.\n\nDoctor: Do you always make yourself throw up after these episodes of eating by yourself?\n\nPatient: Not always, no… but usually. That’s the honest answer.\n\nAfter I eat like that, there’s this immediate panic that sets in, like I have to do something right away before it “counts,” or before the calories settle in, if that makes sense. I know that probably sounds irrational, but in the moment it feels very urgent. So I’ll usually end up going to the bathroom and making myself throw up, because it feels like the only way to undo what just happened.\n\nThere have been times where I haven’t, or I’ve tried not to, but most of the time after those episodes, that’s where my mind goes almost automatically. It’s like the eating part ends, and then the bathroom part becomes the next thing I feel like I have to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about the possibility of gaining weight when you're considering making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much the main thing I’m thinking about, honestly.\n\nIt’s not always like a clear sentence in my head, like “I’m going to gain weight unless I do this,” but that’s the fear underneath it. If I’ve eaten too much, my brain goes straight to, “This is going to show up on me,” or “I’m going to get fat,” and then I feel like I have to do something to stop it.\n\nEven outside of those moments, I get really caught up in small changes. Like if I get on the scale after a work shift and it’s even a pound higher, or if my work pants feel tighter than usual, I’ll spend the whole day worrying about it. I’ll be thinking, “Okay, I’m letting myself go,” or “I need to make up for this somehow.” And then I start planning what I should do differently, or how to fix it.\n\nSo when I’m considering making myself throw up, yeah, gaining weight is definitely part of what I’m afraid of. It feels like I’m trying to undo it before it becomes real.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Usually about once a week… I mean, for at least the last four months, there’s generally been one night most weeks where I eat way more than I meant to when I’m by myself, and then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nSome weeks it might happen more than once, but if I’m trying not to exaggerate, the steady pattern is at least once a week. It’s usually at night, when I’m alone, and once I’ve eaten that much, it feels like I almost automatically go into that next step. I don’t really like admitting how regular it’s become, but that’s the most honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used laxatives or diuretics to try to undo the effects of eating?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, not in the way you’re asking. I haven’t really used laxatives or water pills to try to fix it.\n\nFor me, it’s more that after I eat like that, I usually end up going to the bathroom and making myself throw up. It feels very immediate, like I have to undo it right then, before the calories settle in or before it “counts.” I know that sounds strange saying it out loud, but in that moment it feels like the only option my brain gives me.\n\nDoctor: Is fear of gaining weight something that you think about often, even when you're not eating or considering making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not only right after I’ve eaten or when I’m thinking about throwing up. It’s kind of just running in the background a lot of the time.\n\nLike, if I weigh myself after a shift and the number is even a pound higher, I can get stuck on it for the rest of the day. I’ll tell myself maybe it’s just water or that I’ve been on my feet all day, but it doesn’t really calm me down. My mind goes straight to, “You’re letting yourself get fat,” or “You need to get this under control.”\n\nOr if my work pants feel tighter than usual, even just a little, I notice it constantly. I’ll be bending down or walking around and all I can think about is how they feel on me. Then I start planning how I’m going to make up for it, like what I should do differently or how I can fix it. It’s embarrassing because it can take over the whole day from something that small.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight influence what you eat or how you behave on a typical day when you're not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. Even on days when I’m not having one of those episodes, it can shape the whole day more than I want to admit.\n\nLike if I get on the scale after work and it’s even one pound higher, I don’t just notice it and move on. I’ll keep thinking about it while I’m showering, while I’m trying to relax, even when I’m getting ready for bed. My brain starts going, “You’re letting yourself get fat,” and then I start mentally reviewing everything I ate, or planning how I’m going to make up for it.\n\nSame thing with my work pants. If they feel tighter, even if it’s probably just because I’ve been moving around all day or they came out of the wash weird, I can’t stop noticing it. It makes me feel really uncomfortable in my body, and then I start thinking I need to be stricter or do something to fix it. So, yeah… it affects what I eat and how I act, even on a normal day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever restrict what you eat or skip meals on days when you're not having one of these episodes because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I do start thinking in terms of “making up for it,” even on days when I haven’t had one of those episodes.\n\nIt can start with something really small, like if I weigh myself after a shift and it’s even a pound higher than I expected. I know logically that could be anything — water, being on my feet all day, whatever — but emotionally it feels like proof that I’m losing control. Then I’ll spend the rest of the day kind of obsessing over it, like, “Okay, what do I need to do differently now?” or “How do I fix this before it gets worse?”\n\nOr if my work pants feel tighter, I get really stuck on that. I’ll notice it every time I move, and then my head goes straight to, “You’re letting yourself get fat.” And once that thought is there, it’s hard not to start planning around food — like being stricter, cutting back, trying to compensate somehow. It’s not always some organized plan, but the fear definitely pushes me to change what I’m eating or think about skipping or limiting things because I feel like I need to undo whatever is happening.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect your relationships with friends and family or your ability to work and participate in activities you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does, especially with work. I don’t always want to admit that because I like being seen as someone who’s competent, you know? Like I can handle my job and be professional. But it definitely gets in the way.\n\nThere was this bilingual outreach presentation at work — it was one of those things where I’m supposed to be focused and present and making sure people understand what we’re explaining. And the whole time, or at least a big part of it, I was distracted thinking about how I could leave afterward. Not even in a normal “I’m tired and want to go home” way, more like I was already mentally trying to get out of there because I felt so uncomfortable and anxious about my body and weight.\n\nAnd because my mind was somewhere else, I stumbled through parts of the presentation. I lost my place, repeated myself, and I could feel myself getting flustered. Then of course that made me more self-conscious, because I started worrying that my coworkers noticed I wasn’t really on top of it. I kept thinking, “They can tell something’s wrong,” or “They’re going to think I’m not prepared,” when really I had prepared — I just couldn’t stay focused.\n\nSo, yeah, it affects things. Maybe not every single day in some dramatic way, but enough that it’s interfering with my ability to do my job the way I know I can.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight something that you think about every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, yes. It’s pretty much every day, even if the intensity changes.\n\nSome days it’s not like I’m sitting there actively panicking the whole time, but it’s still kind of running in the background. Like, if I get home from a shift and the scale is even one pound higher, that can just take over my head. I’ll tell myself, “It’s probably water or being on my feet all day,” but then another part of me is already going, “No, you’re letting yourself get fat.” And then I’m thinking about it while I’m doing other things, like replaying what I ate or trying to figure out how I’m going to make up for it.\n\nOr with my work pants — if they feel tighter, that can ruin my whole day. I’ll keep noticing it every time I bend down or walk around, and it turns into this constant reminder that something is wrong with me or that I need to fix it. So even if nothing major happens that day, the fear is still there, and it affects how I think about food and my body almost daily.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social activities or events that you avoid because of your concerns about weight or body image?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, sometimes it’s not even that I’m consciously saying, “I can’t go because of my body,” but that’s what it turns into.\n\nLike, I’ll be getting ready in the bathroom at my apartment, and I’ll catch myself in the mirror, and then everything kind of narrows down to my stomach and my waist. It doesn’t matter if I had a good day otherwise — like even if I did well at the rescue center, handled the animals, got through everything I needed to do — if I look in the mirror and my waist or stomach looks wrong to me, it feels like I failed. Like I wasn’t disciplined enough.\n\nAnd once that feeling hits, going out feels… exposed, I guess. Like I don’t want to be around people or have them see me, even if realistically they’re not looking that closely. It just makes me want to stay home instead of going to something social, because I’m already so stuck in my head about how I look.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the effects of eating after these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that is the main reason. It’s not like I’m doing it because I feel physically sick or something. It’s more like after I eat that way, my mind goes straight to, “You have to undo this before it counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nI usually end up in the bathroom making myself throw up, because in that moment it feels like the only way to stop the calories from… I don’t know, settling in or becoming part of me. I know that probably sounds strange, but it feels really urgent at the time. Like if I don’t do it quickly, then I’ve lost control completely and I’m going to gain weight from it.\n\nSo yes, the fear of gaining weight is a huge part of it. It’s not the only emotion — there’s guilt and panic and shame too — but the reason I try to get rid of it is mostly because I’m scared of what eating like that will do to my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you currently weigh less than 85% of the expected weight for your age and height?\n\nPatient: I’m… not actually sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t really know what the “expected weight” would be for my age and height, or what 85% of that would come out to.\n\nI do weigh myself, but I don’t think about it in those terms, like percentages. I’d probably need you to tell me what number you mean or calculate it. I can tell you my current weight if that helps, but I don’t know off the top of my head whether it’s below that cutoff.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me your current weight?\n\nPatient: I… honestly don’t know the exact number right now. I know that probably sounds like I should, given how much I think about it, but I’m not sure I can give you a current weight that I’d trust.\n\nI do weigh myself sometimes, but it’s not always consistent, and the number kind of gets stuck in my head afterward, so I don’t always want to look directly at it or say it out loud. I’d rather not guess and give you something inaccurate. If you need it, I could check or get weighed here, but off the top of my head, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Can you get weighed here so we can determine your current weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I can do that. I mean, I’m not thrilled about it, if I’m being honest. Getting on a scale here feels kind of different than doing it at home, because then it’s not just me seeing the number and reacting to it privately. It feels more exposed, I guess.\n\nBut I understand why you’re asking, and if it helps figure out where things are medically, then yes, I can be weighed here. I might just need a minute before doing it, and I’d prefer not to have the number said out loud right away if that’s possible. Or maybe you can tell me only if it’s something I need to know. I’m not trying to be difficult — it just tends to really get in my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It feels like I lose the ability to decide in that moment, especially when I’m alone and there’s no one around to interrupt it or see it happening. That’s the main reason I’m here, because it’s started to scare me how out of control it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating by yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I don’t walk away feeling good about it, obviously, but “guilty” is hard because sometimes it’s more like I feel confused or embarrassed, like I’m trying to replay how it even got to that point. I’ll sort of sit there afterward and think, “Why did I do that again?” but I don’t know if that’s guilt exactly or just frustration with myself.\n\nThere’s definitely this heavy feeling after, like I don’t really want to think about it and I don’t want anyone to know. But I don’t always label it as guilt in my head. It’s more like… I feel off, and kind of ashamed maybe, but I’m not sure if I’m using the right word.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s probably the clearest part of it, honestly. Once I’ve started, especially if I’m alone at night, it feels like the stopping part just sort of disappears. I can be thinking very clearly, like, “Put this away now, you’ve had enough, stop opening things,” and at the same time I’m still eating or getting more food.\n\nThat’s what scares me, because it doesn’t feel like a normal decision where I’m just choosing to have extra. It feels more like something has taken over the process, and I’m watching myself keep going even though I don’t want to. I’ll make little rules in my head, like “after this bite” or “after this one thing,” but then I move the line again, and again, and suddenly I’ve eaten way more than I intended.\n\nSo, yeah… I do feel unable to stop once it starts. Not every single time I eat, but during those episodes, yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I know that’s probably something I should be tracking more carefully, but I don’t have a clear answer like, “I gained this many pounds” or “it changed at this exact time.” I notice things more vaguely, like how clothes feel or how I feel looking in the mirror, and even that can be hard to judge because some days I feel like I look completely different than other days.\n\nI don’t think I can confidently say there’s been a significant change that I know is because of these episodes. My weight might move around a bit, but I haven’t been keeping consistent track, and I try not to focus on the number too much because then I can get kind of stuck on it. So… I guess the honest answer is I haven’t noticed a big, clear change over time, at least not one I can point to for sure.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating by yourself happen?\n\nPatient: Usually it’s at least once a week… like, for the last four months or so, I’d say there’s been at least one night most weeks where it happens.\n\nIt’s almost always when I’m alone, and it tends to be at night. I’ll end up eating way more than I meant to — not just “I had a big snack,” but like I keep going past the point I planned to stop. And then afterward I make myself throw up. I don’t like saying that part out loud, but that’s been the pattern.\n\nSome weeks it might be more than once, but if I’m trying to give you the most accurate answer, at minimum it’s usually one night a week for the past several months.\n\nDoctor: Do you always make yourself throw up after these episodes of eating by yourself?\n\nPatient: Not always, no… but usually. That’s the honest answer.\n\nAfter I eat like that, there’s this immediate panic that sets in, like I have to do something right away before it “counts,” or before the calories settle in, if that makes sense. I know that probably sounds irrational, but in the moment it feels very urgent. So I’ll usually end up going to the bathroom and making myself throw up, because it feels like the only way to undo what just happened.\n\nThere have been times where I haven’t, or I’ve tried not to, but most of the time after those episodes, that’s where my mind goes almost automatically. It’s like the eating part ends, and then the bathroom part becomes the next thing I feel like I have to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about the possibility of gaining weight when you're considering making yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much the main thing I’m thinking about, honestly.\n\nIt’s not always like a clear sentence in my head, like “I’m going to gain weight unless I do this,” but that’s the fear underneath it. If I’ve eaten too much, my brain goes straight to, “This is going to show up on me,” or “I’m going to get fat,” and then I feel like I have to do something to stop it.\n\nEven outside of those moments, I get really caught up in small changes. Like if I get on the scale after a work shift and it’s even a pound higher, or if my work pants feel tighter than usual, I’ll spend the whole day worrying about it. I’ll be thinking, “Okay, I’m letting myself go,” or “I need to make up for this somehow.” And then I start planning what I should do differently, or how to fix it.\n\nSo when I’m considering making myself throw up, yeah, gaining weight is definitely part of what I’m afraid of. It feels like I’m trying to undo it before it becomes real.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Usually about once a week… I mean, for at least the last four months, there’s generally been one night most weeks where I eat way more than I meant to when I’m by myself, and then afterward I make myself throw up.\n\nSome weeks it might happen more than once, but if I’m trying not to exaggerate, the steady pattern is at least once a week. It’s usually at night, when I’m alone, and once I’ve eaten that much, it feels like I almost automatically go into that next step. I don’t really like admitting how regular it’s become, but that’s the most honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used laxatives or diuretics to try to undo the effects of eating?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, not in the way you’re asking. I haven’t really used laxatives or water pills to try to fix it.\n\nFor me, it’s more that after I eat like that, I usually end up going to the bathroom and making myself throw up. It feels very immediate, like I have to undo it right then, before the calories settle in or before it “counts.” I know that sounds strange saying it out loud, but in that moment it feels like the only option my brain gives me.\n\nDoctor: Is fear of gaining weight something that you think about often, even when you're not eating or considering making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not only right after I’ve eaten or when I’m thinking about throwing up. It’s kind of just running in the background a lot of the time.\n\nLike, if I weigh myself after a shift and the number is even a pound higher, I can get stuck on it for the rest of the day. I’ll tell myself maybe it’s just water or that I’ve been on my feet all day, but it doesn’t really calm me down. My mind goes straight to, “You’re letting yourself get fat,” or “You need to get this under control.”\n\nOr if my work pants feel tighter than usual, even just a little, I notice it constantly. I’ll be bending down or walking around and all I can think about is how they feel on me. Then I start planning how I’m going to make up for it, like what I should do differently or how I can fix it. It’s embarrassing because it can take over the whole day from something that small.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight influence what you eat or how you behave on a typical day when you're not having one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. Even on days when I’m not having one of those episodes, it can shape the whole day more than I want to admit.\n\nLike if I get on the scale after work and it’s even one pound higher, I don’t just notice it and move on. I’ll keep thinking about it while I’m showering, while I’m trying to relax, even when I’m getting ready for bed. My brain starts going, “You’re letting yourself get fat,” and then I start mentally reviewing everything I ate, or planning how I’m going to make up for it.\n\nSame thing with my work pants. If they feel tighter, even if it’s probably just because I’ve been moving around all day or they came out of the wash weird, I can’t stop noticing it. It makes me feel really uncomfortable in my body, and then I start thinking I need to be stricter or do something to fix it. So, yeah… it affects what I eat and how I act, even on a normal day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever restrict what you eat or skip meals on days when you're not having one of these episodes because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I do start thinking in terms of “making up for it,” even on days when I haven’t had one of those episodes.\n\nIt can start with something really small, like if I weigh myself after a shift and it’s even a pound higher than I expected. I know logically that could be anything — water, being on my feet all day, whatever — but emotionally it feels like proof that I’m losing control. Then I’ll spend the rest of the day kind of obsessing over it, like, “Okay, what do I need to do differently now?” or “How do I fix this before it gets worse?”\n\nOr if my work pants feel tighter, I get really stuck on that. I’ll notice it every time I move, and then my head goes straight to, “You’re letting yourself get fat.” And once that thought is there, it’s hard not to start planning around food — like being stricter, cutting back, trying to compensate somehow. It’s not always some organized plan, but the fear definitely pushes me to change what I’m eating or think about skipping or limiting things because I feel like I need to undo whatever is happening.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect your relationships with friends and family or your ability to work and participate in activities you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does, especially with work. I don’t always want to admit that because I like being seen as someone who’s competent, you know? Like I can handle my job and be professional. But it definitely gets in the way.\n\nThere was this bilingual outreach presentation at work — it was one of those things where I’m supposed to be focused and present and making sure people understand what we’re explaining. And the whole time, or at least a big part of it, I was distracted thinking about how I could leave afterward. Not even in a normal “I’m tired and want to go home” way, more like I was already mentally trying to get out of there because I felt so uncomfortable and anxious about my body and weight.\n\nAnd because my mind was somewhere else, I stumbled through parts of the presentation. I lost my place, repeated myself, and I could feel myself getting flustered. Then of course that made me more self-conscious, because I started worrying that my coworkers noticed I wasn’t really on top of it. I kept thinking, “They can tell something’s wrong,” or “They’re going to think I’m not prepared,” when really I had prepared — I just couldn’t stay focused.\n\nSo, yeah, it affects things. Maybe not every single day in some dramatic way, but enough that it’s interfering with my ability to do my job the way I know I can.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight something that you think about every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, yes. It’s pretty much every day, even if the intensity changes.\n\nSome days it’s not like I’m sitting there actively panicking the whole time, but it’s still kind of running in the background. Like, if I get home from a shift and the scale is even one pound higher, that can just take over my head. I’ll tell myself, “It’s probably water or being on my feet all day,” but then another part of me is already going, “No, you’re letting yourself get fat.” And then I’m thinking about it while I’m doing other things, like replaying what I ate or trying to figure out how I’m going to make up for it.\n\nOr with my work pants — if they feel tighter, that can ruin my whole day. I’ll keep noticing it every time I bend down or walk around, and it turns into this constant reminder that something is wrong with me or that I need to fix it. So even if nothing major happens that day, the fear is still there, and it affects how I think about food and my body almost daily.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social activities or events that you avoid because of your concerns about weight or body image?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, sometimes it’s not even that I’m consciously saying, “I can’t go because of my body,” but that’s what it turns into.\n\nLike, I’ll be getting ready in the bathroom at my apartment, and I’ll catch myself in the mirror, and then everything kind of narrows down to my stomach and my waist. It doesn’t matter if I had a good day otherwise — like even if I did well at the rescue center, handled the animals, got through everything I needed to do — if I look in the mirror and my waist or stomach looks wrong to me, it feels like I failed. Like I wasn’t disciplined enough.\n\nAnd once that feeling hits, going out feels… exposed, I guess. Like I don’t want to be around people or have them see me, even if realistically they’re not looking that closely. It just makes me want to stay home instead of going to something social, because I’m already so stuck in my head about how I look.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the effects of eating after these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that is the main reason. It’s not like I’m doing it because I feel physically sick or something. It’s more like after I eat that way, my mind goes straight to, “You have to undo this before it counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nI usually end up in the bathroom making myself throw up, because in that moment it feels like the only way to stop the calories from… I don’t know, settling in or becoming part of me. I know that probably sounds strange, but it feels really urgent at the time. Like if I don’t do it quickly, then I’ve lost control completely and I’m going to gain weight from it.\n\nSo yes, the fear of gaining weight is a huge part of it. It’s not the only emotion — there’s guilt and panic and shame too — but the reason I try to get rid of it is mostly because I’m scared of what eating like that will do to my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you currently weigh less than 85% of the expected weight for your age and height?\n\nPatient: I’m… not actually sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t really know what the “expected weight” would be for my age and height, or what 85% of that would come out to.\n\nI do weigh myself, but I don’t think about it in those terms, like percentages. I’d probably need you to tell me what number you mean or calculate it. I can tell you my current weight if that helps, but I don’t know off the top of my head whether it’s below that cutoff.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me your current weight?\n\nPatient: I… honestly don’t know the exact number right now. I know that probably sounds like I should, given how much I think about it, but I’m not sure I can give you a current weight that I’d trust.\n\nI do weigh myself sometimes, but it’s not always consistent, and the number kind of gets stuck in my head afterward, so I don’t always want to look directly at it or say it out loud. I’d rather not guess and give you something inaccurate. If you need it, I could check or get weighed here, but off the top of my head, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Can you get weighed here so we can determine your current weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I can do that. I mean, I’m not thrilled about it, if I’m being honest. Getting on a scale here feels kind of different than doing it at home, because then it’s not just me seeing the number and reacting to it privately. It feels more exposed, I guess.\n\nBut I understand why you’re asking, and if it helps figure out where things are medically, then yes, I can be weighed here. I might just need a minute before doing it, and I’d prefer not to have the number said out loud right away if that’s possible. Or maybe you can tell me only if it’s something I need to know. I’m not trying to be difficult — it just tends to really get in my head.\n\nDoctor: Can you step on the scale now so we can get your current weight?\n\nPatient: Okay… yeah. I can do it now.\n\nI might need to take my shoes off and just—give me a second, because I’m already kind of bracing for it more than I probably should. If it’s all right, I’d really prefer not to look at the number while I’m on it, and I’d rather you not say it out loud unless you absolutely need to. I know that sounds particular, but once I hear it, I tend to keep replaying it.\n\nBut yes, I can step on now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t always want to admit that because I like being seen as someone who’s competent, you know? Like I can handle my job and be professional. But it definitely gets in the way.\n\nThere was this bilingual outreach presentation at work — it was one of those things where I’m supposed to be focused and present and making sure people understand what we’re explaining. And the whole time, or at least a big part of it, I was distracted thinking about how I could leave afterward. Not even in a normal “I’m tired and want to go home” way, more like I was already mentally trying to get out of there because I felt so uncomfortable and anxious about my body and weight.\n\nAnd because my mind was somewhere else, I stumbled through parts of the presentation. I lost my place, repeated myself, and I could feel myself getting flustered. Then of course that made me more self-conscious, because I started worrying that my coworkers noticed I wasn’t really on top of it. I kept thinking, “They can tell something’s wrong,” or “They’re going to think I’m not prepared,” when really I had prepared — I just couldn’t stay focused.\n\nSo, yeah, it affects things. Maybe not every single day in some dramatic way, but enough that it’s interfering with my ability to do my job the way I know I can.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight something that you think about every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, yes. It’s pretty much every day, even if the intensity changes.\n\nSome days it’s not like I’m sitting there actively panicking the whole time, but it’s still kind of running in the background. Like, if I get home from a shift and the scale is even one pound higher, that can just take over my head. I’ll tell myself, “It’s probably water or being on my feet all day,” but then another part of me is already going, “No, you’re letting yourself get fat.” And then I’m thinking about it while I’m doing other things, like replaying what I ate or trying to figure out how I’m going to make up for it.\n\nOr with my work pants — if they feel tighter, that can ruin my whole day. I’ll keep noticing it every time I bend down or walk around, and it turns into this constant reminder that something is wrong with me or that I need to fix it. So even if nothing major happens that day, the fear is still there, and it affects how I think about food and my body almost daily.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social activities or events that you avoid because of your concerns about weight or body image?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, sometimes it’s not even that I’m consciously saying, “I can’t go because of my body,” but that’s what it turns into.\n\nLike, I’ll be getting ready in the bathroom at my apartment, and I’ll catch myself in the mirror, and then everything kind of narrows down to my stomach and my waist. It doesn’t matter if I had a good day otherwise — like even if I did well at the rescue center, handled the animals, got through everything I needed to do — if I look in the mirror and my waist or stomach looks wrong to me, it feels like I failed. Like I wasn’t disciplined enough.\n\nAnd once that feeling hits, going out feels… exposed, I guess. Like I don’t want to be around people or have them see me, even if realistically they’re not looking that closely. It just makes me want to stay home instead of going to something social, because I’m already so stuck in my head about how I look.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you try to undo the effects of eating after these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that is the main reason. It’s not like I’m doing it because I feel physically sick or something. It’s more like after I eat that way, my mind goes straight to, “You have to undo this before it counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nI usually end up in the bathroom making myself throw up, because in that moment it feels like the only way to stop the calories from… I don’t know, settling in or becoming part of me. I know that probably sounds strange, but it feels really urgent at the time. Like if I don’t do it quickly, then I’ve lost control completely and I’m going to gain weight from it.\n\nSo yes, the fear of gaining weight is a huge part of it. It’s not the only emotion — there’s guilt and panic and shame too — but the reason I try to get rid of it is mostly because I’m scared of what eating like that will do to my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you currently weigh less than 85% of the expected weight for your age and height?\n\nPatient: I’m… not actually sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t really know what the “expected weight” would be for my age and height, or what 85% of that would come out to.\n\nI do weigh myself, but I don’t think about it in those terms, like percentages. I’d probably need you to tell me what number you mean or calculate it. I can tell you my current weight if that helps, but I don’t know off the top of my head whether it’s below that cutoff.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me your current weight?\n\nPatient: I… honestly don’t know the exact number right now. I know that probably sounds like I should, given how much I think about it, but I’m not sure I can give you a current weight that I’d trust.\n\nI do weigh myself sometimes, but it’s not always consistent, and the number kind of gets stuck in my head afterward, so I don’t always want to look directly at it or say it out loud. I’d rather not guess and give you something inaccurate. If you need it, I could check or get weighed here, but off the top of my head, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Can you get weighed here so we can determine your current weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I can do that. I mean, I’m not thrilled about it, if I’m being honest. Getting on a scale here feels kind of different than doing it at home, because then it’s not just me seeing the number and reacting to it privately. It feels more exposed, I guess.\n\nBut I understand why you’re asking, and if it helps figure out where things are medically, then yes, I can be weighed here. I might just need a minute before doing it, and I’d prefer not to have the number said out loud right away if that’s possible. Or maybe you can tell me only if it’s something I need to know. I’m not trying to be difficult — it just tends to really get in my head.\n\nDoctor: Can you step on the scale now so we can get your current weight?\n\nPatient: Okay… yeah. I can do it now.\n\nI might need to take my shoes off and just—give me a second, because I’m already kind of bracing for it more than I probably should. If it’s all right, I’d really prefer not to look at the number while I’m on it, and I’d rather you not say it out loud unless you absolutely need to. I know that sounds particular, but once I hear it, I tend to keep replaying it.\n\nBut yes, I can step on now."
  }
